\displaystyle\frac{1-\mu^{4}}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\mu^{4}-1)(\mu^{4}+7)}=:re^{\pm i\theta}.

where
r=\sqrt{2(1-\mu^{4})}
and
\cos\theta=\sqrt{(1-\mu^{4})/8}
or
\sin\theta=\sqrt{(\mu^{4}+7)/8}
. Eq. (
SF.170
) coincides with (i) the large-
q
expansion of the exact finite-
q
result of the TopSFF in Eq. (
SI.241
) at
t=1
, and (ii) the exact large-
q
calculation of the TopSFF for the RPM at
t=1
in the absence of discrete time translational symmetry in Eq. (
SJ.274
).

F.4
TopSFF with engineered parity inversion axes

Different momentum sectors of TopSFF, in particular the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector containing the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking transition, can be accessed by engineering the coupling gates along the parity inversion axes. We focus on two choices: no coupling and period-2 driving along these axes. Comparing the corresponding TopSFF allows us to isolate the
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
contribution.

F.4.1
No coupling

Here we compute the TopSFF in Eq. (
SF.165
) exactly for RPM with discrete time translational symmetry, but without coupling along the parity inversion axes. The generalized Boltzmann factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
remains the same, while the boundary states in Eq. (
SE.66
) in momentum space are given by

\widetilde{\phi}(a;k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=i^{\delta_{a,1}}\sqrt{2t^{3}}\delta_{n_{b},0}\delta_{n_{c},0}\delta_{n_{d},0},

(SF.171)

Since the boundary states lie entirely in the momentum sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(0,0,0)
, the TopSFF without coupling along parity inversion axes can be evaluated as

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}=8t^{3}\mathsf{K}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}\,,

(SF.172)

where
\mathsf{K}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}
is given in (
SF.158
).
This case is useful for removing contributions in TopSFF associated with the zero momentum sector.

F.4.2
Period-2 driving

Here we compute the TopSFF in Eq. (
SF.165
) exactly for RPM with discrete time translational symmetry, but with period-2 driving along the parity inversion axes. The generalized Boltzmann factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
remains the same, while the boundary states in Eq. (
SE.66
) in momentum space are given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(a;k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=

\displaystyle i^{\delta_{a,1}}\left[\mu^{2t}\sqrt{2t^{3}}\,\delta_{n_{b},0}\delta_{n_{c},0}\delta_{n_{d},0}+(1-\mu^{2t})\sqrt{\frac{t}{2}}\;\delta_{n_{b}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ t),\,0}\;\delta_{n_{c}+n_{d}+n_{b}/2\ (\mathrm{mod}\ t),0}\right],

(SF.173)

which has support in the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric sectors
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})\in\{(0,k,-k),(\pi,k,\pi-k)\}
. Defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2}}\Big[1+(2t-1)\mu^{2t}\Big],\qquad\mathcal{N}_{\star}:=\sqrt{\frac{t}{2}}\,(1-\mu^{2t})\,,

(SF.174)

the TopSFF with period-2 driving along parity inversion axes can be evaluated as

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}=\mathcal{N}^{2}_{0}\mathsf{K}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}+(t-1)\mathcal{N}^{2}_{\star}\mathsf{K}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}+\mathcal{N}^{2}_{\star}\Big[2\mathsf{K}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}+(t-2)\mathsf{K}_{\pi_{\mathrm{b}}}\Big].

(SF.175)

where
\mathsf{K}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}
,
\mathsf{K}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}
,
\mathsf{K}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}
and
\mathsf{K}_{\pi_{\mathrm{b}}}
are respectively given in (
SF.158
), (
SF.160
), (
SF.162
) and (
SF.164
).

F.4.3
Isolating
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
sector by engineering parity inversion axes

We extract the TopSFF directly in the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector by computing TopSFF in finite-
q
via dual spatial propagation of the quantum circuit, as discussed in Secs.
I.1
and
L
.
However, in principle, TopSFF of
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
and its
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking transition can be extracted by engineering the parity inversion axes.
More specifically, comparing the no-coupling boundary condition with period-2 driving allows one to separate the dominant contributions between the
k_{b}=0
sector and the
k_{b}=\pi
sector. As shown in Fig.
S14
, among the momentum sectors accessed by period-2 driving, namely
(0,0,0)
,
(0,k,-k)
,
(\pi,\pi,0)
, and
(\pi,k,\pi-k)
, only the
(0,0,0)
and
(\pi,\pi,0)
sectors contain eigenvalues with modulus larger than unity. The contributions from the remaining sectors therefore decay exponentially with system size
L
. Consequently, at large
L
, the contribution from the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector can in principle be isolated from the period-2 TopSFF by subtracting the corresponding zero-momentum contribution,

\mathsf{K}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}=\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{p=2}-\beta\,\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{no\text{-}coupling}},

(SF.176)

where
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{p=2}
denotes TopSFF with period-2 driving along the parity-inversion axes,
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{no\text{-}coupling}}
denotes TopSFF without coupling along these axes, and
\beta
is chosen to fit the zero momentum sector contribution. This subtraction isolates the
(\pi,\pi,0)
contribution up to terms that are exponentially small in
L
. In practice, however, we extract the TopSFF directly in the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector by computing TopSFF in finite-
q
via dual spatial propagation of the quantum circuit, as discussed in Secs.
I.1
and
L
.

Appendix G
Emergent
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric dimers

In this appendix, we first review the two-mode
\mathcal{PT}
dimer, and then show how the TopSFF generalized Boltzmann factor reduces to a canonical
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric dimer. This gives an interpretation of the
\mathcal{PT}
unbroken regime,
\mathcal{PT}
broken regime, and the exceptional point in terms of Gaussian/non-Gaussian tDW detuning and conversion.

G.1
Review of
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric dimers

Consider a single complex amplitude
\psi(t)
evolving under a generator
H
as

i\frac{d}{dt}\psi=H\psi.

(SG.177)

If
H=i\gamma
with
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\frac{d}{dt}\psi=\gamma\psi\quad\Rightarrow\quad\psi(t)=e^{\gamma t}\psi(0).

(SG.178)

Thus
\gamma>0
gives exponential growth of amplitude, which is often referred to as “gain”. If instead
H=-i\gamma
, then

\frac{d}{dt}\psi=-\gamma\psi\quad\Rightarrow\quad\psi(t)=e^{-\gamma t}\psi(0),

(SG.179)

which leads to exponential decay of amplitude, which is referred to as “loss”.

Consider the canonical
\mathcal{PT}
dimer

H=\begin{pmatrix}+i\gamma&J\\
J&-i\gamma\end{pmatrix},

(SG.180)

where
J,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. The diagonal entries
\pm i\gamma
represent balanced gain/loss, while
J
coherently couples the two modes. In particular, we have
\operatorname{tr}H=i\gamma-i\gamma=0
,
so the gain and loss are exactly balanced at the level of the trace. The eigenvalues of the
\mathcal{PT}
dimer are

E_{\pm}=\pm\sqrt{J^{2}-\gamma^{2}},

(SG.181)

and a right eigenvector
\psi=(u,v)^{T}
of the eigenvalue
E
satisfies

(i\gamma-E)u+Jv=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\frac{v}{u}=\frac{E-i\gamma}{J}.

(SG.182)

The Hamiltonian (
SG.180
) satisfies the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry

(\mathcal{PT})H(\mathcal{PT})^{-1}=H,\qquad\mathcal{P}=\sigma_{x},

(SG.183)

and with
\mathcal{T}
being complex conjugation. However, individual eigenvectors need not be invariant under
\mathcal{PT}
.
If
H|\psi\rangle=E|\psi\rangle
, then

H(\mathcal{PT}|\psi\rangle)=E^{*}(\mathcal{PT}|\psi\rangle),

(SG.184)

so
\mathcal{PT}
maps an eigenvector at
E
to an eigenvector at
E^{*}
.

The phases of the model are given as follow.

•

Unbroken
\mathcal{PT}
phase (
|\gamma|<|J|
): real spectrum and balanced modes.

In this strong coupling regime with
E_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
, the time evolution is governed by pure phases,
e^{-iE_{\pm}t}
, and there is no exponential growth/decay in time: the eigenmodes are dynamically balanced.
The right eigenvectors can be chosen as
\mathcal{PT}
eigenstates

\mathcal{PT}|\psi_{\pm}\rangle=e^{i\theta_{\pm}}|\psi_{\pm}\rangle.

(SG.185)

Using
E^{2}=J^{2}-\gamma^{2}
in (
SG.182
) gives

\left|\frac{v}{u}\right|=\frac{|E-i\gamma|}{|J|}=\frac{\sqrt{E^{2}+\gamma^{2}}}{|J|}=\frac{\sqrt{J^{2}}}{|J|}=1.

(SG.186)

Thus each eigenvector has equal weight on the gain and loss sites (up to a relative phase): the eigenvectors samples both sites so that net gain/loss cancels.

•

Broken
\mathcal{PT}
phase (
|\gamma|>|J|
): complex-conjugate spectrum and polarized modes.

Write

E_{\pm}=\pm i\kappa,\qquad\kappa:=\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-J^{2}}>0.

(SG.187)

Then time evolution contains exponentials,
e^{-iE_{\pm}t}=e^{\pm\kappa t}
, so one eigenmode amplifies while the other eigenmode decays.
\mathcal{PT}
cannot leave an eigenvector invariant, since it maps
E_{\pm}\mapsto E_{\pm}^{*}=E_{\mp}
. In other words,

\mathcal{PT}|\psi_{+}\rangle\propto|\psi_{-}\rangle,\qquad\mathcal{PT}|\psi_{-}\rangle\propto|\psi_{+}\rangle.

(SG.188)

The ratio of the components of eigenvector becomes

\frac{v}{u}=\frac{i\kappa-i\gamma}{J}=i\,\frac{\kappa-\gamma}{J},

(SG.189)

so generically
\bigl|v/u\bigr|\neq 1
, i.e., the right eigenvectors become polarized toward one site/mode. This eigenvector polarization is the signature accompanying
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking.

•

Exceptional point (
|\gamma|=|J|
): coalescence of eigenvalues and eigenvectors.

At threshold,
E_{+}=E_{-}=0
and the two eigenvalues merge. Generically the corresponding eigenvectors coalesce, so
H
becomes non-diagonalizable and is represented by a Jordan block. This coalescence of eigenvalues and eigenvectors defines the exceptional point.

G.2
TopSFF and
\mathcal{PT}
dimers

According to Proposition
E.3
, in the momentum space, the generalized Boltzmann factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})
has
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry with complex conjugation
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{P}:=\sigma_{z}
, if
k_{b}+k_{c}+k_{d}\equiv 0\pmod{\pi}
. At such symmetric points, the generalized Boltzmann factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})
is a 2-by-2 matrix in
a
-space with the form

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}&\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{10}&\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{A}&i\mathsf{B}\\
i\mathsf{B}&\mathsf{C}\end{pmatrix}=d_{0}\,\mathbb{1}+d_{z}\,\sigma_{z}+i\mathsf{B}\,\sigma_{x},\qquad d_{0}:=\frac{\mathsf{A}+\mathsf{C}}{2},\qquad d_{z}:=\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}.

(SG.190)

where
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C}\in\mathbb{R}
, and we have left labels of momentum sector implicit. In terms of the temporal domain wall basis,

\{\ket{{\mathrm{G}}}:=\ket{a=0;k_{b},k_{c},k_{d}}\quad\text{(Gaussian tDW)},\qquad\ket{{\mathrm{nG}}}:=\ket{a=1;k_{b},k_{c},k_{d}}\quad\text{(non-Gaussian tDW)}\,\},

(SG.191)

the entry of the Boltzmann factor has the following interpretation:

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}\equiv\mathsf{A}
: Amplitude for the Gaussian tDW to remain as Gaussian tDW,

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}\equiv\mathsf{C}
: Amplitude for the non-Gaussian tDW to remain as non-Gaussian tDW,

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}\equiv i\mathsf{B}
: Amplitude for the DW type conversion with a
\pi/2
phase.

Now introduce a unitary transformation

U:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad U\sigma_{z}U^{\dagger}=\sigma_{x},\qquad U\sigma_{x}U^{\dagger}=\sigma_{z}.

(SG.192)

Then the rotated TopSFF Boltzmann factor is the canonical
\mathcal{PT}
dimer up to a trace shift,

U\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}U^{\dagger}=d_{0}\,\mathbb{1}+d_{z}\,\sigma_{x}+i\mathsf{B}\,\sigma_{z}=d_{0}\,\mathbb{1}+\begin{pmatrix}i\mathsf{B}&\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}\\
\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}&-i\mathsf{B}\end{pmatrix}\equiv d_{0}\,\mathbb{1}+\begin{pmatrix}+i\gamma&J\\
J&-i\gamma\end{pmatrix},

(SG.193)

where the coupling strength and gain/loss rates are determined respectively by

J:=d_{z}=\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}}{2},\qquad\gamma:=\mathsf{B}=-i\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}.

(SG.194)

Note that after the mapping,
U\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}U^{\dagger}
is invariant under the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry with complex conjugation
\mathcal{T}
and the standard parity symmetry operator
U\mathcal{P}U^{\dagger}=\sigma_{x}
. The basis is now

\left\{\ket{+}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{{\mathrm{G}}}+\ket{{\mathrm{nG}}}\right),\qquad\ket{-}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{{\mathrm{G}}}-\ket{{\mathrm{nG}}}\right)\right\},

(SG.195)

The eigenvalues of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
are

\lambda_{\pm}=\frac{\mathsf{A}+\mathsf{C}}{2}\pm\sqrt{\Delta}
with the transition-controlling discriminant

\Delta:=J^{2}-\gamma^{2}=\left(\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}\right)^{2}-\mathsf{B}^{2}=\left(\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}}{2}\right)^{2}-\left(-i\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}\right)^{2},

(SG.196)

i.e., the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry-breaking transition is controlled by whether the Gaussian/non-Gaussian tDW conversion amplitude exceeds the detuning between the Gaussian and non-Gaussian tDW propagation amplitudes.

TopSFF can be obtained by evaluating
\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}}\varphi
with the vector
\varphi=(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})^{T}
. As discussed in
F.1
, using the
Cayley-Hamilton theorem on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
, we can write

\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}}\varphi=A(L_{\mathrm{e}})\,\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\varphi+B(L_{\mathrm{e}})\varphi^{T}\varphi\,,

(SG.197)

where

A(L_{\mathrm{e}}):=\frac{\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}-\lambda_{-}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}}{\lambda_{+}-\lambda_{-}},\qquad B(L_{\mathrm{e}}):=\frac{\lambda_{+}\lambda_{-}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}-\lambda_{-}\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}}{\lambda_{+}-\lambda_{-}}.

(SG.198)

and, in terms of components of
\varphi
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\,\varphi=\mathsf{A}\,\varphi_{1}^{2}+\mathsf{C}\,\varphi_{2}^{2}+2i\mathsf{B}\,\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2},\qquad\varphi^{T}\varphi=\varphi_{1}^{2}+\varphi_{2}^{2}\,.

(SG.199)

For a given momentum sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})
where
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry is present, the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking transition has the following interpretation:

•

Unbroken
\mathcal{PT}
phase (
\Delta>0
and
\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}\right|<\left|\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}}{2}\right|
): Polarization of Gaussian/non-Gaussian tDWs.

In the rotated basis the right eigenvectors can be chosen as
\mathcal{PT}
eigenstates, i.e. they are balanced across the effective gain/loss modes.
In the unrotated tDW basis, the eigenvectors are dominated by
\ket{{\mathrm{G}}}
or
\ket{{\mathrm{nG}}}
, so the propagation eigenmodes do not exhibit substantial coherent conversion between Gaussian and non-Gaussian tDWs.

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
are distinct. Take the convention where
|\lambda_{+}|>|\lambda_{-}|
. From
(
SG.198
),

A(L_{\mathrm{e}})=c\,\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}-1},\qquad c:=\frac{1-(\lambda_{-}/\lambda_{+})^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}}{1-(\lambda_{-}/\lambda_{+})}\approx\frac{1}{1-(\lambda_{-}/\lambda_{+})},

(SG.200)

and similarly
B(L_{\mathrm{e}})=\mathcal{O}\left(\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}-1}\right)
. Hence

\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}}\varphi
grows or decays exponentially without oscillations with the dominant real eigenvalue.

•

Broken
\mathcal{PT}
phase (
\Delta<0
and
\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}\right|>\left|\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}}{2}\right|
): Hybridization of Gaussian/non-Gaussian tDWs.

In the rotated basis the eigenvalues form a complex-conjugate pair and the eigenvectors are not
\mathcal{PT}
eigenstates (
\mathcal{PT}
exchanges them).
In the unrotated tDW basis, this polarization manifests as hybridization between

\ket{{\mathrm{G}}}
and
\ket{{\mathrm{nG}}}
: each propagation eigenmode involves nontrivial coherent hybridization of the Gaussian and non-Gaussian tDW sectors, with one mode effectively amplified and the other mode attenuated under repeated multiplication by
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
.

The eigenvalues
\lambda_{\pm}
form a complex-conjugate pair. Write

\lambda_{\pm}=d_{0}\pm i\sqrt{|\Delta|}=r\,e^{\pm i\theta},\qquad r:=|\lambda_{\pm}|=\sqrt{d_{0}^{2}+|\Delta|},\qquad\theta:=\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{|\Delta|}}{d_{0}}\right).

(SG.201)

In particular,

A(L_{\mathrm{e}})=\frac{\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}-\lambda_{-}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}}{\lambda_{+}-\lambda_{-}}=r^{L_{\mathrm{e}}-1}\,\frac{\sin(L_{\mathrm{e}}\theta)}{\sin\theta},

(SG.202)

and
B(L_{\mathrm{e}})=\mathcal{O}(r^{L_{\mathrm{e}}-1})
. Therefore,

\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}}\varphi
has an envelope
r^{L_{\mathrm{e}}-1}
with oscillations
set by
\sin(L_{\mathrm{e}}\theta)/\sin\theta
.

•

Exceptional point (
\Delta=0
and and
\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}\right|=\left|\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}-\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}}{2}\right|
): onset of hybridization and mode coalescence.

At
\Delta=0
the two eigenvalues coalesce, and generically the two eigenvectors merge into a
single eigenvector in the Jordan form. This is the
\mathcal{PT}
threshold where the tDW sector hybridization switches on non-analytically.

The eigenvalue
\lambda_{+}=\lambda_{-}=d_{0}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
is generically non-diagonalizable. Taking the coalescing limit in (
SG.198
) yields

A(L_{\mathrm{e}})=L_{\mathrm{e}}\,d_{0}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}-1},\qquad B(L_{\mathrm{e}})=(1-L_{\mathrm{e}})\,d_{0}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}}.

(SG.203)

Thus
\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\,L_{\mathrm{e}}}\varphi
acquires the characteristic Jordan non-diagonality, i.e., an extra factor of
L_{\mathrm{e}}
relative to the generic cases. See
F.1.1
for the extraction of the linear scalling in
L_{\mathrm{e}}
from TopSFF.

Appendix H
TopSFF and exceptional points (EP)

In this appendix, we analyse exceptional points (EP) of the generalized Boltzmann factor in the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric momentum sectors. Section
H.1
solves the exceptional-point condition in the four selected momentum sectors
(0,k,-k)
,
(0,0,0)
,
(\pi,\pi,0)
, and
(\pi,k,\pi-k)
. Section
H.2
presents the resulting EP data. Section
H.3
presents the signatures of eigenvalue and eigenvector coalescence, including the Jordan-block contribution exposed by boundary-resolved TopSFFs.

H.1
EP in selected momentum sectors

We study exceptional points (EPs) of the generalized Boltzmann factor in the
\mathcal{PT}
-symmetric sectors. In these sectors, the Boltzmann factor takes the form

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{A}&i\mathsf{B}\\
i\mathsf{B}&\mathsf{C}\end{pmatrix},

(SH.204)

with eigenvalues

\lambda_{\pm}=\frac{\mathsf{A}+\mathsf{C}}{2}\pm\sqrt{\Delta},\qquad\Delta:=\left(\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}\right)^{2}-\mathsf{B}^{2}.

(SH.205)

Exceptional points occur when the discriminant vanishes,
\Delta=0
, equivalently

\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=\pm 2\mathsf{B}.

(SH.206)

For each
\mathcal{PT}
-symmetric sector, we define the branch polynomials

P^{\pm}(\mu):=\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}\mp 2\mathsf{B},

(SH.207)

so that the EP condition is
P^{\pm}(\mu)=0
. Below we identify the corresponding values of
\mu
for which
\Delta=0
. See Sec.
G.2
for further discussion of the associated
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking transition.

H.1.1
EP for
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(0,k,-k)

For the sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(0,k,-k)
with
k\neq 0
, the matrix elements are given in Eq. (
SF.159
).

(+)
branch:
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=+2\mathsf{B}
.

On this branch, the geometric sums cancel, and

P^{+}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(\mu)=\mu^{t-1}(\mu^{2}-1)\bigl(2t\mu^{4}-\mu^{2}+1\bigr).

(SH.208)

The factor
\mu^{2}-1
gives the boundary solution
\mu=1
, while for
t\geq 2
the factor
\mu^{t-1}
gives the boundary solution
\mu=0
. The remaining quadratic has discriminant
1-8t<0
for all
t\geq 1
, so it has no real root. Hence there is no EP with
\mu\in(0,1)
on the
(+)
branch.

(-)
branch:
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=-2\mathsf{B}
.

On this branch,

\displaystyle P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(\mu)=

\displaystyle 8\sum_{m=0}^{t-1}\mu^{4m}-16\sum_{m=t}^{2t-1}\mu^{2m}-\mu^{4t-4}+2\mu^{4t-2}+(6t-1)\mu^{4t}+2t\mu^{4t+2}.

(SH.209)

One checks that

P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(1)=0,\qquad P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(0)=8\quad(t\geq 2),\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(1)=4t>0.

(SH.210)

For
t\geq 2
,
P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(\mu)
is negative just to the left of
\mu=1
and positive at
\mu=0
. Therefore, by continuity there is at least one non-trivial root in
(0,1)
. Thus the
(-)
branch admits an interior EP. At large
t
, the interior root approaches
\mu=1
as

\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}(t)=1-\frac{1}{t^{2}}+O(t^{-3}),\qquad t\gg 1.

(SH.211)

In Fig.
S13
, we plot
P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}
whose roots determine
\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}(t)
.

Figure S13:

Exceptional points in the
(0,k,-k)
and
(\pi,\pi,0)
sectors at large
q
.

Left and middle panels show
P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{b}}}(\epsilon)
and
P^{+}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\epsilon)
, respectively, as functions of
\epsilon\equiv-2\ln\mu
, with
t=2,\ldots,10
shown from yellow to blue. For each
t\geq 2
, a non-trivial root with
\mu\in(0,1)
signals an exceptional point. The inset in the middle panel enlarges the root region for the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector. Right panel: exceptional-point locations versus
t
for the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector and the
(0,k,-k)
sector. As
t
increases, the exceptional point in the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector approaches
\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}=\log 2
, see (
SH.217
).

H.1.2
EP for
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(0,0,0)

For the sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(0,0,0)
, the matrix elements are given in Eq. (
F.2.1
).

(+)
branch:
\mathsf{A}_{0}-\mathsf{C}_{0}=+2\mathsf{B}_{0}
.

On this branch, the geometric sums cancel, and

P^{+}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)=\mu^{t-1}(\mu^{2}-1)\Bigl[2t(t-1)\mu^{4}+(2t-1)\mu^{2}+1\Bigr].

(SH.212)

The factor
\mu^{2}-1
gives the boundary solution
\mu=1
, while for
t\geq 2
the factor
\mu^{t-1}
gives the boundary solution
\mu=0
. The quadratic in
\mu^{2}
has discriminant
(2t-1)^{2}-8t(t-1)=1+4t(1-t)\leq 1
, which is negative for all
t\geq 2
, so there is no real root. Hence there is no EP with
\mu\in(0,1)
on the
(+)
branch.

(-)
branch:
\mathsf{A}_{0}-\mathsf{C}_{0}=-2\mathsf{B}_{0}
.

The EP condition is

P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)=0
,
with

\displaystyle P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)={}

\displaystyle 8\sum_{m=0}^{t-1}\mu^{2m}+16(t-1)\sum_{m=t}^{2t-1}\mu^{m}+\bigl(8t(t-1)^{2}-2t^{2}+4t-1\bigr)\mu^{2t}-\mu^{2t-2}-2(t-1)\mu^{2t-1}+2t(t-1)\mu^{2t+3}.

(SH.213)

This can be rearranged as

\displaystyle P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)={}

\displaystyle 8\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\mu^{2m}+7\mu^{2t-2}+16(t-1)\sum_{m=t}^{2t-2}\mu^{m}+14(t-1)\mu^{2t-1}

(SH.214)

\displaystyle+\bigl(8t(t-1)^{2}-2t^{2}+4t-1\bigr)\mu^{2t}+2t(t-1)\mu^{2t+3}.

All coefficients are non-negative, and in fact strictly positive for
t\geq 2
. Therefore
P^{-}_{0_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)>0
for
\mu\in(0,1)
, and the
(-)
branch has no interior solution.

H.1.3
EP for
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)

For the sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
, the matrix elements are given in Eq. (
SF.161
).

(+)
branch:
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=+2\mathsf{B}
.

The
(+)
branch simplifies to

P^{+}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)=\mu^{4t-4}(1-\mu^{2})\Bigl(2t\mu^{4}-(t-1)\mu^{2}-1\Bigr).

(SH.215)

Besides the boundary solutions
\mu=0
(for
t\geq 2
) and
\mu=1
, the only interior EP comes from the quadratic equation

2t\mu^{4}-(t-1)\mu^{2}-1=0.

(SH.216)

Solving for
\mu^{2}
gives

\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{(t-1)+\sqrt{(t-1)^{2}+8t}}{4t}}\;\xrightarrow{t\gg 1}\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,.

(SH.217)

In terms of
\epsilon
with
\mu=e^{-\epsilon/2}
, we have

\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}(t)=\log\!\left[\frac{\sqrt{(t-1)^{2}+8t}-(t-1)}{2}\right]\;\xrightarrow{t\gg 1}\;\log 2\,.

(SH.218)

See Fig.
S13
for the locations of the EP for
t
30.
We are particularly interested in the case of
t=2
at large
q
, where
\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}(t=2)=0.8002
and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}(t=2)=-2\log\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}(t=2)=0.4457
, since we can compare these prediction with our exact computation at finite
q
.

(-)
branch:
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=-2\mathsf{B}
.

Using Eqs. (
SF.161
) and (
SF.157
), the branch polynomial

\displaystyle P^{-}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)={}

\displaystyle 8\sum_{m=0}^{t-1}\mu^{4m}+8(t-2)\sum_{m=t}^{2t-3}\mu^{2m}+(8t-17)\mu^{4t-4}+(7t-14)\mu^{4t-2}

(SH.219)

\displaystyle-\bigl(8t^{2}-11t+1\bigr)\mu^{4t}-2t\,\mu^{4t+2}.

It can be shown that
P^{-}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu)
is positive for
\mu\in(0,1)
, and hence this branch gives no additional root in
\mu\in(0,1)
.

H.1.4
EP for
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,k,\pi-k)

For the sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,k,\pi-k)
with
k\neq 0,\pi
, the matrix elements are given in Eq. (
SF.163
).

(+)
branch:
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=+2\mathsf{B}
.

Using Eq. (
SF.163
), one finds

P^{+}_{\pi_{\mathrm{b}}}(\mu)=-\mu^{4t-4}(1-\mu^{2})^{2}.

(SH.220)

Hence the
(+)
branch has only the boundary solution
\mu=1
, and no EP in the interior
\mu\in(0,1)
.

(-)
branch:
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}=-2\mathsf{B}
.

On this branch, using the definitions of
A_{t-1}
and
C_{t}
in (
SF.157
), we obtain

P^{-}_{\pi_{\mathrm{b}}}(\mu)=7\mu^{4t-4}(1-\mu^{2})^{2}+8\sum_{r=2}^{t-1}\mu^{4t-4r}(1-\mu^{2r})^{2}+8(1-\mu^{2t})^{2},

(SH.221)

which has positive terms, leading to
P^{-}_{\pi_{\mathrm{b}}}(\mu)>0
for
\mu\in(0,1)
. Therefore, the
(-)
branch has no interior solution. Hence the
(\pi,k,\pi-k)
sector has no exceptional point for
\mu\in(0,1)
.

H.2
EP data

\mathcal{PT}

and

.
\Re\lambda_{\pm}
. For

H.3
Eigenvalue and eigenvector coalescence at EP

).

).

Jordan block non-diagonality of TopSFF at the EP at large

.

We plot the boundary-resolved TopSFF

(\mathsf{K}_{00}+\mathsf{K}_{11})/\lambda_{\mathrm{c}}^{L/2-2}

in the
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
sector as a function of the physical system size
L
for

(left) and

(right), where
\lambda_{c}(\mu):=\operatorname{Tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}/2=(\lambda_{+}+\lambda_{-})/2
reduces to the coalesced eigenvalue
\lambda_{\mathrm{EP}}
at
\epsilon=\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}
. The curves correspond to different values of
\epsilon\in[\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}-\delta\epsilon,\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}+\delta\epsilon]
, with

for

and

for

.

L_{\mathrm{e}}
and

(dashed black line), as predicted in Eq. (
SF.155
).
At the EP, the two eigenvalues of the generalized Boltzmann factor coalesce and the matrix becomes non-diagonalizable, producing the Jordan-block scaling

\mathsf{K}_{00}-\mathsf{K}_{11}\propto L_{\rm eff}\lambda^{L_{\rm eff}-1}
, resulting in a linear growth with
L
after dividing the TopSFF by the exponential envelope. Away from
\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}
, the eigenvalues split, the matrix is diagonalizable, and the curves depart from the linear EP form.

In this section, we discuss the signatures of exceptional points (EPs) of the
\mathcal{PT}
-symmetric generalized Boltzmann factor, including eigenvalue coalescence and eigenvector coalescence, as diagnosed by eigenvector self-orthogonality and the divergence of the Petermann factor. We focus on the sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
, although the discussion applies more generally to any
\mathcal{PT}
-symmetric momentum sector.
In the momentum sector
(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
, the generalized Boltzmann factor is
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric and takes the form

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{A}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}&i\,\mathsf{B}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}\\
i\,\mathsf{B}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}&\mathsf{C}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}\end{pmatrix},

(SH.222)

where the matrix entries are defined in Eq. (
SF.161
), and we use the shorthand
\pi_{\mathrm{e}}\equiv(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})=(\pi,\pi,0)
for this momentum sector.
At an exceptional point, the two eigenvalues and their corresponding eigenvectors coalesce. This is a second order exceptional point (EP2), since the algebraic multiplicity is 2, while the geometric multiplicity is 1. In what follows, we examine both eigenvalue coalescence and eigenvector coalescence in the vicinity of the EP.

•

Square root scaling of eigenvalue coalescence.

The eigenvalues are

\lambda_{\pm}=\frac{\operatorname{Tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}\pm\sqrt{\Delta_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}}{2},\qquad\Delta_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}:=\operatorname{Tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}^{2}-4\det\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}=(\mathsf{A}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}-\mathsf{C}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}})^{2}-4\mathsf{B}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}^{2}.

(SH.223)

At the exceptional point, the discriminant vanishes so that the two eigenvalues coalesce,

\Delta_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\lambda_{+}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})=\lambda_{-}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}).

(SH.224)

Expanding near the exceptional point, we have generically

\lambda_{\pm}(\mu)=\lambda_{\mathrm{EP}}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Delta^{\prime}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})}\,\sqrt{\mu-\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}}+O(\mu-\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}),\qquad\Delta^{\prime}_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\Delta_{\pi_{\mathrm{e}}}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\neq 0,

(SH.225)

Thus, the eigenvalue splitting, i.e., the difference between the two eigenvalues, exhibits the characteristic square-root scaling near the EP,

\lambda_{+}-\lambda_{-}\propto\sqrt{\Delta(\mu)}\propto\sqrt{|\mu-\mu_{\mathrm{EP}}|}\propto\sqrt{|\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mathrm{EP}}|}.

(SH.226)

Note that this square-root eigenvalue splitting occurs on both sides of the EP. In the unbroken
\mathcal{PT}
phase, the two eigenvalues split along the real axis, whereas in the broken
\mathcal{PT}
phase they split along the imaginary axis. See Fig.
S14
and Fig.
S17
for square root eigenvalue at the EP ar large
q
for various
t
.

•

Square root scaling of eigenvector coalescence.

A convenient choice of right and left eigenvectors, satisfying

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\pi,\pi,0)\,r_{\pm}=\lambda_{\pm}r_{\pm}
and

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\pi,\pi,0}^{\dagger}\,\ell_{\pm}=\lambda_{\pm}^{*}\,\ell_{\pm}
, is

r_{\pm}:=\begin{pmatrix}i\mathsf{B}\\
\lambda_{\pm}-\mathsf{A}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\ell_{\pm}:=\begin{pmatrix}-i\mathsf{B}\\
\lambda_{\pm}^{*}-\mathsf{A}\end{pmatrix}.

(SH.227)

–

Eigenvector self-orthogonality.

Define the biorthogonal overlap

\mathcal{O}_{\pm}:=|\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}|.

(SH.228)

Away from the exceptional point, the left and right eigenvectors may be chosen biorthogonal and normalized so that

\ell_{n}^{\dagger}r_{m}=\delta_{nm}
. For the explicit choice in Eq. (
SH.227
), one finds

\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}=(\lambda_{\pm}-\mathsf{A})^{2}-\mathsf{B}^{2}
.
Using

\lambda_{\pm}-\mathsf{A}=\frac{\mathsf{C}-\mathsf{A}\pm\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}
, and

\Delta=(\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C})^{2}-4\mathsf{B}^{2}
,
we obtain

\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\Delta\mp(\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C})\sqrt{\Delta}\right].

(SH.229)

At an EP2,
\Delta=0
, we have

\mathcal{O}_{\pm}(\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})=0\qquad\text{at EP},

(SH.230)

i.e., the coalesced eigenvector is self-orthogonal at the EP.
Expanding around the EP, we write

\Delta(\mu)=\Delta^{\prime}(\mu_{\rm EP})(\mu-\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})+O((\mu-\mu_{\mathrm{EP}})^{2})

with
\Delta^{\prime}(\mu_{\rm EP})\neq 0
.
Therefore,
around the EP, we have

\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}=\mp\frac{\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}}{2}\sqrt{\Delta}\,\Big[1+O(\sqrt{\Delta})\Big].

(SH.231)

Provided
\mathsf{A}-\mathsf{C}\neq 0
at
\mu=\mu_{\rm EP}
, which is generically the case, we obtain

\mathcal{O}_{\pm}(\mu)=\left|\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}\right|\propto\left|\sqrt{\Delta(\mu)}\right|\propto\sqrt{\left|\mu-\mu_{\rm EP}\right|}\propto\sqrt{\left|\epsilon-\epsilon_{\rm EP}\right|}.

(SH.232)

i.e., the biorthogonal overlap vanishes with a square root exponent at a generic EP2. See Fig.
S18
for the vanishing biorthogonal overlap at the EP in the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector at large-
q
.

–

Divergence of the Petermann factor.
A standard measure of eigenvector coalescence in non-Hermitian systems is the
Petermann factor

\mathcal{P}_{\pm}(\mu):=\frac{\|\ell_{\pm}\|^{2}\,\|r_{\pm}\|^{2}}{|\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}|^{2}},

(SH.233)

where
r_{\pm}
and
\ell_{\pm}
are respectively the right and left
eigenvectors, and
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Euclidean norm. This quantity is invariant under independent rescalings of
r_{\pm}
and
\ell_{\pm}
. At a generic second-order exceptional point, we have
\Delta(\mu_{\rm EP})=0

and
A-C\neq 0
, and the norms
\|r_{\pm}\|
and
\|\ell_{\pm}\|
remain finite and non-zero as

\mu\to\mu_{\rm EP}
. Using (
SH.232
), we obtain

\mathcal{P}_{\pm}(\mu)\propto\frac{1}{|\ell_{\pm}^{\dagger}r_{\pm}|^{2}}\propto\frac{1}{|\Delta(\mu)|}\propto\frac{1}{|\mu-\mu_{\rm EP}|}\propto\frac{1}{|\epsilon-\epsilon_{\rm EP}|},

(SH.234)

i.e. the Petermann factor diverges with exponent
-1
at a generic EP2. See Fig.
S19
for the divergent Petermann factor
at the EP in the
(\pi,\pi,0)
sector at large
q
.

Appendix I
Exact TopSFF with spatially extended defects at finite
q

In this appendix, we present an exact finite-
q
evaluation of the TopSFF with a spatially extended swap defect. We first formulate the computation for finite
q
and small
t
using an ensemble-averaged transfer matrix, and then give a analytic evaluation at
t=1
and finite
q
.

I.1
Exact finite-
q
symbolic evaluation of TopSFF

We now describe the exact finite-
q
symbolic computation of the TopSFF,
K_{\text{top}}(t,L)=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{U}\otimes\hat{U}^{*})]=\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
, with
[\hat{\mathcal{D}},(\hat{U}\otimes\hat{U}^{*})]=0
,
where the spatially extended topological defect is
\hat{\mathcal{D}}=\hat{S}\otimes\mathbb{1}
.
Here
\hat{S}
is the global swap operator, which reverses the ordering of the
L
qudits according to
\hat{S}\ket{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{L-1},a_{L}}=\ket{a_{L},a_{L-1},\dots,a_{2},a_{1}}
, with
a_{i}=1,\dots,q
. We analyse
K_{\text{top}}
in a generic many-body quantum chaotic system satisfying
[\hat{\mathcal{D}},\hat{U}\otimes\hat{U}^{*}]=0
, i.e. the system possesses parity inversion symmetry.
Such a system is minimally realised by the Random Phase Model (RPM) with parity inversion symmetry and discrete time translation symmetry, defined in App.
A.2.1
, i.e.,
\hat{U}=\hat{U}_{\mathrm{f\text{-}p\text{-}RPM}}
.
The local Hilbert-space dimension is denoted by
q
.
Instead of generating an ensemble of quantum circuits and computing the averaged
TopSFF, this approach computes the generalized Boltzmann factor after the ensemble average over the quantum circuits.
In particular, this computation keeps
all
permutations in Eq. (
SE.54
)
at finite
q
, as opposed to retaining only the large-
q
Mickey Mouse diagrams.
For a single folded site, the two permutations appearing in the Weingarten
contraction are labelled by the pair
(\sigma,\tau)\in S_{2t}\times S_{2t}
.
The Kronecker deltas generated by Eq. (
SE.54
) identify
the local time indices into closed loops. We denote the resulting set of loops by

\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\tau)
, and its cardinality by

\ell(\sigma,\tau):=|\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\tau)|
. At finite
q
, each loop
carries an explicit colour index
a\in\{1,\dots,q\}
. The basis state for a
single folded site is labelled by
\ket{\rho}\equiv\ket{\sigma,\tau;\mathbf{a}}
,
with
(\sigma,\tau)\in S_{2t}\times S_{2t}
, and

\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell(\sigma,\tau)})\in\{1,\ldots,q\}^{\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\tau)}
.

In order to perform the average over the 2-site coupling gate, consider the colour index
\Theta_{\rho}(x)
associated to each endpoint,
see dotted lines along
b
-,
c
- and
d
-loops in
Fig.
S5
(e), for a given configuration
\rho
. The endpoint
label
x
runs over
1,\ldots,4t
. The first
2t
labels are the endpoints
associated to
\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
, and the labels
2t+1,\ldots,4t
are the
endpoints associated to
\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
.
For a given pair
(\sigma,\tau)
, the loop partition

\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\tau)
is obtained by following the contractions around the
folded site. Let
R_{U}
be the cyclic rotation acting on the
2t
endpoints
associated to
\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
, and let
R_{U^{\dagger}}
be the cyclic
rotation acting separately on the two
t
-blocks associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
. Explicitly, for
m\in\{1,\ldots,2t\}
,

R_{U}(m)=\begin{cases}m+1,&1\leq m<2t,\\
1,&m=2t,\end{cases}

while

R_{U^{\dagger}}(m)=\begin{cases}m+1,&1\leq m<t,\\
1,&m=t,\\
m+1,&t+1\leq m<2t,\\
t+1,&m=2t.\end{cases}

Starting from an endpoint
y\in\{1,\ldots,2t\}
associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
, one first reaches the endpoint
2t+\tau(y)
associated
to
\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
, and then returns to an endpoint associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
. The resulting map on the

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
endpoints is

\Phi_{\sigma,\tau}(y):=R_{U}^{-1}\sigma^{-1}R_{U^{\dagger}}\tau(y).

The loops are the orbits of
\Phi_{\sigma,\tau}
, together with their
associated endpoints on
\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
. Explicitly, we write

L_{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\cup\{\,2t+\tau(y):y\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\,\},\qquad\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\in{\rm Cyc}(\Phi_{\sigma,\tau}),

and the set of all loops are given by
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\tau)=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{\ell(\sigma,\tau)}\}
. The total number of coloured basis states is

N(t,q)=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{2t}}q^{\ell(\sigma,\tau)}=(2t)!\sum_{\pi\in S_{2t}}q^{C(\pi)}=(2t)!\prod_{r=0}^{2t-1}(q+r),

where
C(\pi)
denotes the number of cycles of
\pi
. For example, one finds, for
t=2
,

\{N(2,q)\}_{q=2}^{5}=\{2880,\;8640,\;20160,\;40320\},

and for
t=3
,

\{N(3,q)\}_{q=2}^{3}=\{3628800,\;14515200\}.

The two-site coupling comes from averaging the random diagonal phases in the
coupling gates.
Define the colour function as

\Theta_{\rho}(x)=a_{\alpha}

if and only if

x\in L_{\alpha}.

Let

\rho_{1}=(\sigma_{1},\tau_{1};\mathbf{a}_{1})
and

\rho_{2}=(\sigma_{2},\tau_{2};\mathbf{a}_{2})

be two adjacent one-site basis states. We write

\Theta_{\rho_{1}}(x),\Theta_{\rho_{2}}(x)\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
for the colours
carried by endpoint
x
in the loop configurations
\rho_{1}
and

\rho_{2}
, respectively. The endpoints are split into those associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
,
x=1,\ldots,2t
, and those associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
,
x=2t+1,\ldots,4t
. The phase average depends only on
the imbalance of colour pairs between the endpoints associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]
and the endpoints associated to

\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
. Then the diagonal phase average gives the factor

\displaystyle D_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}(\mu)=\mu^{Q(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})},\qquad Q(\rho_{1},\rho_{2}):=\sum_{r,s=1}^{q}\left[\sum_{x=1}^{2t}\delta_{\Theta_{\rho_{1}}(x),r}\delta_{\Theta_{\rho_{2}}(x),s}-\sum_{x=2t+1}^{4t}\delta_{\Theta_{\rho_{1}}(x),r}\delta_{\Theta_{\rho_{2}}(x),s}\right]^{2}.

(SI.235)

From this expression, we see that the interaction does not depend on the
absolute values of the loop colours, but only on which loop colours coincide with which other loop colours. We will use this fact to compress the size of
\mathcal{B}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
below. Combining the one-site Weingarten function
\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma^{-1}\tau,q)
with the two-site phase average gives the exact finite-
q
generalized Boltzmann factor as an
N(t,q)
-dimensional transfer matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=\sqrt{\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma_{1}^{-1}\tau_{1},q)}\,D_{\rho_{1}\rho_{2}}(\mu)\,\sqrt{\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma_{2}^{-1}\tau_{2},q)}.

(SI.236)

Note that, at finite
q
, the generalized Boltzmann factor
\mathcal{B}
acts on the full permutation-and-colour basis, and therefore has a much larger matrix dimension than the large-
q
generalized Boltzmann factor, which is restricted to the leading Mickey Mouse diagrams.
The generalized Boltzmann factor can have signs and phases, and as a result, the emergent physics is non-Hermitian.
Although each Weingarten weight is increasingly suppressed in the large-
q
expansion as
t
increases, the matrix dimension of the generalized Boltzmann factor
\mathcal{B}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
grows rapidly with
t
. After summing over the finite-
q
basis states in the transfer-matrix contraction, the unnormalized
TopSFF scales as
\overline{K_{\text{top}}}=O(q^{-L_{\mathrm{e}}})
.

The TopSFF in a momentum sector is obtained by contracting powers of the
position-space transfer matrix
\mathcal{B}
between momentum-resolved boundary
states via
\overline{K_{\text{top}}}=\phi_{k}^{T}\mathcal{B}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\phi_{k}
. We consider
a single momentum sector
k=(k_{b},k_{c},k_{d})
, where

k_{b}=2\pi n_{b}/(2t)
,
k_{c}=2\pi n_{c}/t
, and
k_{d}=2\pi n_{d}/t
, with

n_{b}=0,1,\ldots,2t-1
and
n_{c},n_{d}=0,1,\ldots,t-1
.
Let
(\sigma_{abcd},\tau_{abcd})
, defined in
Eq. (
SE.59
), denote the Mickey Mouse diagrams
labelled by
a=0,1
,
b=1,\ldots,2t
, and
c,d=1,\ldots,t
. We define the
phase function

\displaystyle\Psi_{k}(\sigma,\tau)=\begin{cases}\exp\!\left[i\left(bk_{b}+ck_{c}+dk_{d}\right)\right],&\text{if }(\sigma,\tau)=(\sigma_{abcd},\tau_{abcd}),\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(SI.237)

The corresponding boundary state has components

\braket{\sigma,\tau;\mathbf{a}|\phi_{k}}=\sqrt{\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma^{-1}\tau,q)}\,\Psi_{k}(\sigma,\tau)
. This boundary state is independent of the explicit loop-colour assignment
\mathbf{a}
. The colour degrees of freedom enter only through the bulk transfer matrix
\mathcal{B}
, while

\phi_{k}
selects the desired coherent superposition of Mickey Mouse permutation-pair sectors.

Lastly, the colour structure in Eq. (
SI.235
) allows an exact compression. Since the bulk interaction and the momentum boundary states are invariant under a global relabelling of the
q
colours,
one may replace explicit colour assignments by their equality patterns. Let
m=\ell(\sigma,\tau)
, and write
\mathcal{L}(\sigma,\tau)=\{L_{1},\ldots,L_{m}\}
. Instead of assigning an explicit colour
a_{\alpha}\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
to each loop
L_{\alpha}
, we assign an equality pattern, namely a partition
\lambda=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\}
of the loop labels
\{1,\ldots,m\}
, with

1\leq r\leq\min(m,q)
. Two loops have the same colour if and only if their labels belong to the same block of
\lambda
, i.e.

a_{\alpha}=a_{\beta}
if
\alpha,\beta\in P_{j}
for some
j
.
For example, for
m=3
, the partition
\lambda=\{\{1,2\},\{3\}\}
represents all explicit colour assignments with

a_{1}=a_{2}\neq a_{3}
, such as
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=(1,1,2)
,
(3,3,1)
, or

(q,q,1)
. The compressed one-site basis state is labelled by

\ket{\rho}_{\rm orb}=\ket{\sigma,\tau;\lambda}_{\rm orb}.

For a partition
\lambda=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\}
, define the
block-colour function
\widehat{\Theta}_{\sigma,\tau;\lambda}(x)=j
if
x\in L_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in P_{j}
. Choosing a physical colours amounts to choosing
r
distinct colours using the map
\iota:\{1,\ldots,r\}\to\{1,\ldots,q\}
with distinct values
\iota(j)=c_{j}
, and set

\Theta_{\sigma,\tau;\lambda,\iota}(x)=\iota\!\left(\widehat{\Theta}_{\sigma,\tau;\lambda}(x)\right)
.
For a fixed equality pattern with
r
colour blocks, the number of explicit colour assignments represented by this orbit is

w(\lambda)=(q)_{r}=q(q-1)\cdots(q-r+1)
.
The number of possible equality patterns for
m
loops is the restricted Bell number

B_{m}^{(q)}=\sum_{r=1}^{\min(m,q)}S(m,r),

where
S(m,r)
is the Stirling number of the second kind. Hence an
m
-loop
sector contributes
B_{m}^{(q)}
orbit states rather than
q^{m}
explicit-colour
states. The corresponding basis size can be evaluated as

N_{\rm orb}(t,q)=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{2t}}B_{\ell(\sigma,\tau)}^{(q)}=(2t)!\sum_{m=1}^{2t}c(2t,m)\sum_{r=1}^{\min(m,q)}S(m,r),

where
c(n,m)
is the unsigned Stirling number of the first kind.
For fixed
t
, the orbit-compressed basis size
N_{\rm orb}(t,q)
stops growing once
q\geq 2t
and becomes independent of
q
, since an
m
-loop sector can use at most
m\leq 2t
distinct colours. This allows us to compute TopSFF for arbitrary large
q
for the case of
t=2
. For example, one finds for
t=2
,

\{N_{\rm orb}(2,q)\}_{q=2}^{5}=\{1440,1728,1752,1752\},

and for
t=3
,

\{N_{\rm orb}(3,q)\}_{q=2}^{5}=\{1814400,2678400,2894400,2916000\}
. In a normalized orbit basis,

\ket{\sigma,\tau;\lambda}_{\rm orb}=w(\lambda)^{-1/2}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{\rm Orb}(\lambda)}\ket{\sigma,\tau;\mathbf{a}},

the matrix element of the compressed transfer matrix is

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\rm orb}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{w(\lambda_{1})w(\lambda_{2})}}\sqrt{\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma_{1}^{-1}\tau_{1},q)}\sqrt{\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma_{2}^{-1}\tau_{2},q)}\sum_{\iota_{1}}\sum_{\iota_{2}}\mu^{Q\left(\sigma_{1},\tau_{1};\lambda_{1},\iota_{1};\sigma_{2},\tau_{2};\lambda_{2},\iota_{2}\right)},

(SI.238)

where
\rho_{j}=(\sigma_{j},\tau_{j};\lambda_{j})
, and the sums are over all choices
of distinct physical colours for the blocks of
\lambda_{j}
, equivalently over
maps
\iota_{j}:\{1,\ldots,r_{j}\}\to\{1,\ldots,q\}
with distinct values. The
exponent
Q
is the same phase exponent as in
Eq. (
SI.235
), but with
\Theta_{\rho_{j}}(x)
replaced
by
\Theta_{\sigma_{j},\tau_{j};\lambda_{j},\iota_{j}}(x)
. In the same normalized orbit basis, the momentum-resolved boundary state becomes

\braket{\sigma,\tau;\lambda|\phi_{k}}_{\rm orb}=\sqrt{w(\lambda)}\sqrt{\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma^{-1}\tau,q)}\Psi_{k}(\sigma,\tau),

(SI.239)

such that
\overline{K_{\text{top}}}=\phi_{\mathrm{orb},k}^{T}\mathcal{B}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}_{\rm orb}\phi_{\mathrm{orb},k}
.

I.2
Exact finite-
q
analytical evaluation of TopSFF at
t=1

For
t=1
at finite-
q
, we evaluate exactly the TopSFF,
K_{\text{top}}(t,L)=\mathrm{Tr}[\hat{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{U}\otimes\hat{U}^{*})]=\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
, for the Random Phase Model (RPM) with parity inversion symmetry, defined in App.
A.2.1
. At
t=1
, imposing time translation symmetry makes no difference, since there is only a single time step. The computation follows the colour-orbit construction above. For
t=1
, the explicit colour basis has size
N(1,q)=2q(q+1)
, while the compressed basis has size
N_{\rm orb}(1,q)=6
for

q\geq 2
. The boundary state further restricts the problem to a
three-dimensional invariant subspace. Diagonalizing the corresponding
finite-
q
generalized Boltzmann factor gives

\displaystyle\overline{K_{\mathrm{top}}}(t=1,L_{\mathrm{e}},\mu,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(1-\mu^{4}+\rho)}{(q+1)(\mu^{4}+7-\rho)}\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}+\frac{2(1-\mu^{4})}{(q+1)\rho}\lambda_{-}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}},

(SI.240)

where
\lambda_{\pm}(\mu,q)=(1-\mu^{4}\pm\rho)/[2(q+1)]
,

\rho(\mu)=\sqrt{(\mu^{4}-1)(\mu^{4}+7)}
,
\mu=e^{-\epsilon/2}\in[0,1]
, and

L_{\mathrm{e}}=L/2-1
for even
L
(the case for odd
L
can be analysed easily). For
0\leq\mu<1
,
\rho=i\sqrt{(1-\mu^{4})(\mu^{4}+7)}
, so
\lambda_{\pm}
form a complex-conjugate pair, and the
t=1
finite-
q

Boltzmann factor is in the broken
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric regime. Writing

\lambda_{\pm}=\sqrt{2(1-\mu^{4})}\,e^{\pm i\theta}/(q+1)
, with

\cos\theta=\sqrt{(1-\mu^{4})/8}
and

\sin\theta=\sqrt{(\mu^{4}+7)/8}
, the result becomes

\overline{K_{\mathrm{top}}}(t=1,L_{\mathrm{e}},\mu,q)=-\frac{4\sqrt{1-\mu^{4}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{4}+7}}\,\Big(\sqrt{2(1-\mu^{4})}\Big)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\sin(L_{\mathrm{e}}\theta)\left(\frac{1}{q+1}\right)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}+1},

(SI.241)

which makes the oscillatory dependence on
L_{\mathrm{e}}
explicit. Note that
\overline{K_{\mathrm{top}}}(t=1,L_{\mathrm{e}},\mu,q)=O(q^{-L_{\mathrm{e}}-1})=O(q^{-L/2})
. The finite-
q
answer has the same
\mu
- and
L_{\mathrm{e}}
-dependence as the
large-
q
result. The only difference is the replacement

q^{-(L_{\mathrm{e}}+1)}
to
(q+1)^{-(L_{\mathrm{e}}+1)}
. Therefore, at
t=1
, finite
q
therefore preserves the

\mathcal{PT}
symmetric structure and the oscillatory behaviour in
L
, but renormalizes the overall large-
q
factor from
q^{-L_{\mathrm{e}}-1}
to
(q+1)^{-L_{\mathrm{e}}-1}
. The finite-
q
TopSFF at
t=1
is plotted as a function of the system size

L
in Fig.
S21
.

Figure S21:
Exact TopSFF at
t=1
at finite
q
.

TopSFF (
SI.241
) of the parity symmetric RPM at
\epsilon=1
(with or without discrete time translational symmetry) against system size
L
at
t=1
from
q=2
(yellow) to
q=6
(blue).

Appendix J
TopSFF with spatially extended topological defects without time translational symmetry

In this appendix, we exactly compute the TopSFF with a spatially extended defect for the RPM in the absence of discrete time translation symmetry. We show that the TopSFF still gives rise to an emergent non-Hermitian
\mathcal{PT}
-symmetric single-particle problem for a temporal domain wall. However, any
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking transitions are pushed towards the boundaries of parameter space, corresponding to zero or infinite interaction strength, as
t
increases in the pre-Heisenberg regime. This contrasts with the time-translation symmetric case, where a
\mathcal{PT}
transition can occur at finite interaction strength for arbitrary
t
.

J.1
Boltzmann factor
\mathcal{B}
in position space

Figure S22:
Emergent non-Hermitian single-particle model
, as defined in (
SJ.247
). The single particle residing in the top (bottom) chain corresponds to the Gaussian (non-Gaussian) temporal domain wall. The intra-chain and inter-chain hopping terms are Hermitian and non-Hermitian respectively. We illustrate hopping amplitudes up to
O(\mu^{2})
.

Here we exactly compute TopSFF for a spatially extended defect for the RPM in the absence of discrete time translational symmetry. As in the case with time translational symmetry, we compute the TopSFF

K_{\text{top}}(t,L)=\mathrm{Tr}\!\left[\hat{\mathcal{D}}(\hat{U}\otimes\hat{U}^{*})\right]=\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{S}\hat{U}]\operatorname{Tr}[\hat{U}^{\dagger}]
, where

\hat{\mathcal{D}}=\hat{S}\otimes\mathbb{1}

with global swap operator
\hat{S}
.
\hat{U}
is defined in (
SA.4
), which commutes with
\hat{D}
, but does not have discrete time translational symmetry, i.e., the RPM is temporally random and globally parity-inversion symmetric.
Upon ensemble averaging over (
SA.4
), the TopSFF
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}
can be expressed in terms of a transfer matrix, which describes a single-particle non-Hermitian hopping problem:

\displaystyle\overline{K}_{\mathrm{top}}

\displaystyle=:q^{-L_{\mathrm{e}}-1}\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}+O(q^{-{L_{\mathrm{e}}}-2}),

(SJ.242)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}

\displaystyle=\phi^{T}\mathcal{B}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\phi:=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\phi(\sigma_{1})\left[\mathcal{B}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\right]_{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\phi(\sigma_{2}),

where
\mathcal{B}
and
\phi
are respectively the generalized Boltzmann factor encoding the many-body interactions and the boundary state in the folded representation. The effective length is
L_{\mathrm{e}}=L/2-1
for even
L
. For odd
L
, TopSFF can be evaluated analogously.
The local TopSFF configurations are labelled by
\sigma=(a,b)
in the folded representation.
Here
a\in\{0,1\}
labels the type of temporal domain wall (tDW): Gaussian tDW with
a=0
consists only of Gaussian contractions, while non-Gaussian tDW with
a=1
involves both Gaussian and non-Gaussian contractions. Further,
b\in\{1,2,\dots,2t\}
labels the position along the local length-
2t
loop in
\operatorname{tr}[\hat{D}\hat{U}]
in the folded representation. There are in total
4t
local TopSFF configurations. Writing
\sigma_{i}=(a_{i},b_{i})
and
\Delta b:=(b_{2}-b_{1})\pmod{2t}
, the generalized Boltzmann factor can be written as

\mathcal{B}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=\big[\mathcal{B}_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\Delta b)\big]_{a_{1},a_{2}=0,1}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{B}_{00}&\mathcal{B}_{01}\\
\mathcal{B}_{10}&\mathcal{B}_{11}\end{bmatrix},

(SJ.243)

where the matrix elements are

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{00}(\Delta b)=

\displaystyle\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,0}+\mu^{4t-4}\,\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mu^{4\ell-4}\Big(\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,\ell}+\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,-\ell}\Big),

(SJ.244)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{11}(\Delta b)=

\displaystyle-\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,0}-\mu^{4t}\,\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,t}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mu^{4\ell}\Big(\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,\ell}+\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,-\ell}\Big),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{01}(\Delta b)=

\displaystyle i\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\mu^{4\ell-4}\Big(\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,-\ell}+\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,\ell-1}\Big),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{10}(\Delta b)=

\displaystyle i\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\mu^{4\ell-4}\Big(\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,\ell}+\delta^{(2t)}_{\Delta b,1-\ell}\Big).

where
\delta^{(2t)}_{x,y}=1
if
x\equiv y\ (\mathrm{mod}\ 2t)
and
0
otherwise.

It is natural to represent
\mathcal{B}
as a single-particle hopping problem of the temporal domain wall in second-quantized form by introducing creation and annihilation operators
\hat{c}_{A,j}^{(\dagger)}
and
\hat{c}_{B,j}^{(\dagger)}
,

|a=0,b\rangle:=\hat{c}_{A,b}^{\dagger}|0\rangle,\qquad|a=1,b\rangle:=\hat{c}_{B,b-\frac{1}{2}}^{\dagger}|0\rangle,

(SJ.245)

with periodic boundary condition
b\equiv b+2t
. Here
|0\rangle
denotes the vacuum state annihilated by all annihilation operators, and
A
and
B
label the two sublattices. In this basis,

H=\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\mathcal{B}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\,|\sigma_{1}\rangle\langle\sigma_{2}|,\qquad\mathcal{B}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}):=\langle\sigma_{1}|H|\sigma_{2}\rangle.

(SJ.246)

Note that such a Hamiltonian representation can also be made for Eq. (
SE.67
) in the presence of discrete time translational symmetry. In terms of creation and annihilation operators, in the absence of time translational symmetry, the Hamiltonian can then be written as

\displaystyle H=

\displaystyle\,\,H_{A}+H_{B}+H_{AB}\,,

(SJ.247)

\displaystyle H_{A}=

\displaystyle\,\,\sum_{j=1}^{2t}\Big[\hat{c}_{A,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{A,j}+\mu^{4t-4}\hat{c}_{A,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{A,j+t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mu^{4\ell-4}\big(\hat{c}_{A,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{A,j+\ell}+\hat{c}_{A,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{A,j-\ell}\big)\Big]\,,

\displaystyle H_{B}=

\displaystyle\,\,-\sum_{j=1/2}^{2t-1/2}\Big[\hat{c}_{B,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{B,j}+\mu^{4t}\hat{c}_{B,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{B,j+t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mu^{4\ell}\big(\hat{c}_{B,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{B,j+\ell}+\hat{c}_{B,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{B,j-\ell}\big)\Big]\,,

\displaystyle H_{AB}=

\displaystyle\,\,i\sum_{j=1}^{2t}\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\mu^{4\ell-4}\Big(\hat{c}_{A,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{B,j-\ell-\frac{1}{2}}+\hat{c}_{A,j}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{B,j+\ell-\frac{3}{2}}+\hat{c}_{B,j-\ell-\frac{1}{2}}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{A,j}+\hat{c}_{B,j+\ell-\frac{3}{2}}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{A,j}\Big).

Here
\hat{c}_{A,j}^{(\dagger)}
with
j=1,2,\dots,2t
and
\hat{c}_{B,j}^{(\dagger)}
with
j=1/2,3/2,\dots,2t-1/2
act on the two legs of the ladder, with periodic identification
\hat{c}^{(\dagger)}_{X,2tm+j}=\hat{c}^{(\dagger)}_{X,j}
for
X=A,B
.

J.2
Boltzmann factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
in momentum space

Utilizing the translational invariance of
{\mathcal{B}}
, we can express the TopSFF in terms of the generalized Boltzmann factor in momentum space as

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}=\phi^{T}\mathcal{B}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\phi=\sum_{k}\widetilde{\phi}(-k)^{T}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\,\widetilde{\phi}(k),

(SJ.248)

where the discrete Fourier transforms of
\phi
and
{\mathcal{B}}
are defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(a;k):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{b=0}^{2t-1}e^{ikb}\,\phi(a,b),

(SJ.249)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{a_{1}a_{2}}(k):=

\displaystyle\sum_{\Delta b=0}^{2t-1}\mathcal{B}_{a_{1}a_{2}}(\Delta b)\,e^{-ik\Delta b}.

where
k=2\pi n/(2t)
with
n=0,1,\dots,2t-1
.
A compatible momentum-space convention for the annihilation operators is

\hat{c}_{A,b}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{k}e^{-ikb}\hat{c}_{A,k},\qquad\hat{c}_{A,k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{b=1}^{2t}e^{ikb}\hat{c}_{A,b},

(SJ.250)

and

\hat{c}_{B,b-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{k}e^{-ikb}\hat{c}_{B,k},\qquad\hat{c}_{B,k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{b=1}^{2t}e^{ikb}\hat{c}_{B,b-\frac{1}{2}}.

(SJ.251)

where
k=\pi n/t
,
n=0,1,\dots,2t-1
.
With these conventions, the hopping Hamiltonian becomes

H=\sum_{k}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}_{A,k}^{\dagger}&\hat{c}_{B,k}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}_{A,k}\\
\hat{c}_{B,k}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)=\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}(k)&\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}(k)\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{10}(k)&\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}(k)\end{pmatrix}.

(SJ.252)

Evaluating the Fourier sums gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{00}(k)

\displaystyle=1+\mu^{4t-4}e^{-itk}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mu^{4\ell-4}\cos(\ell k)=:\mathsf{A}(k),

(SJ.253)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{11}(k)

\displaystyle=-\Big[1+\mu^{4t}e^{-itk}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{t-1}\mu^{4\ell}\cos(\ell k)\Big]=:\mathsf{C}(k),

(SJ.254)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{01}(k)

\displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\mu^{4\ell-4}\Big(e^{i\ell k}+e^{-i(\ell-1)k}\Big)=i\,e^{ik/2}\mathsf{B}(k),

(SJ.255)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{10}(k)

\displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}\mu^{4\ell-4}\Big(e^{-i\ell k}+e^{i(\ell-1)k}\Big)=i\,e^{-ik/2}\mathsf{B}(k),

(SJ.256)

where

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{A}(k)&i\,e^{ik/2}\mathsf{B}(k)\\
i\,e^{-ik/2}\mathsf{B}(k)&\mathsf{C}(k)\end{pmatrix},

(SJ.257)

and after evaluating the geometric sums,

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\mu^{4})(1-\mu^{4}+2\cos k)-(1-\mu^{8})\mu^{4t-4}e^{-itk}}{1-2\mu^{4}\cos k+\mu^{8}},

(SJ.258)

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}(k)

\displaystyle=-\frac{(1-\mu^{8})\bigl(1-\mu^{4t}e^{-itk}\bigr)}{1-2\mu^{4}\cos k+\mu^{8}},

(SJ.259)

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(1-\mu^{4})\bigl(1-\mu^{4t}e^{-itk}\bigr)\cos(k/2)}{1-2\mu^{4}\cos k+\mu^{8}}.

(SJ.260)

In this basis, the off-diagonal entries differ by the phase factors
e^{\pm ik/2}
. We can remove the off-diagonal phases by a momentum-dependent unitary transformation,

\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{c}_{A,k}\\
\hat{c}_{B,k}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{d}_{A,k}\\
\hat{d}_{B,k}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{k}=U(k)\,\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{k},\qquad U(k):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&e^{ik/2}\end{pmatrix}.

(SJ.261)

In terms of
\hat{d}
, the
B
-sublattice Fourier transform becomes

\hat{c}_{B,b-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{k}e^{-ik(b+\frac{1}{2})}\hat{d}_{B,k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{k}e^{-ikj}\hat{d}_{B,k},\qquad j:=b+\frac{1}{2}.

(SJ.262)

In terms of the
\hat{d}
,

H=\sum_{k}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{d}_{A,k}^{\dagger}&\hat{d}_{B,k}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k)\begin{pmatrix}\hat{d}_{A,k}\\
\hat{d}_{B,k}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k)=U(k)\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)\,U(k)^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf{A}(k)&i\mathsf{B}(k)\\
i\mathsf{B}(k)&\mathsf{C}(k)\end{pmatrix}.

(SJ.263)

Since
k=\pi n/t
implies
e^{-itk}=(-1)^{n}
, the coefficients
\mathsf{A}(k)
,
\mathsf{B}(k)
, and
\mathsf{C}(k)
are real. Therefore
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k)
is in the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric dimer form; see Sec.
G.2
.

Figure S23:
Eigenvalues of Boltzmann factor for
t=4
in the absence of time translational symmetry.

Complex eigenvalues of the generalized Boltzmann factor of
k\in\{0,1,2,\dots,2t-1\}
.
\Re\lambda_{\pm}
are plotted in red and blue solid lines.
\Im\lambda_{\pm}
are plotted in red and blue dashed lines. All sectors are
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric. Exceptional points are shown in the sectors
k=\pi/2,3\pi/4,5\pi/4,3\pi/2
.
Unlike the case with time translational symmetry,
|\lambda_{\pm}|
do not exceed
1
, see
S14
.

J.3
Properties of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)
and
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry

Here we describe the properties of the momentum space generalized Boltzmann factor in Eq. (
SJ.257
) and Eq. (
SJ.263
):

•

Reality of
\det\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)
and
\operatorname{tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)
, and spectral pairing.
Since the allowed momenta are

k=\pi n/t

with
n=0,1,\dots,2t-1
, we have

e^{-itk}=(-1)^{n}\in\mathbb{R}
.
It follows directly from Eqs. (
SJ.257
)–(
SJ.263
) that

\mathsf{A}(k),\mathsf{B}(k),\mathsf{C}(k)\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\mathsf{A}(-k)=\mathsf{A}(k),\quad\mathsf{B}(-k)=\mathsf{B}(k),\quad\mathsf{C}(-k)=\mathsf{C}(k).

(SJ.264)

As a consequence,

\operatorname{tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)=\mathsf{A}(k)+\mathsf{C}(k)\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\det\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)=\mathsf{A}(k)\mathsf{C}(k)+\mathsf{B}(k)^{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

(SJ.265)

Hence the characteristic polynomial has real coefficients, and the eigenvalues are

\lambda_{\pm}(k)=\frac{\operatorname{tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)\pm\sqrt{\Delta(k)}}{2},\qquad\Delta(k):=\big[\mathsf{A}(k)-\mathsf{C}(k)\big]^{2}-4\mathsf{B}(k)^{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

(SJ.266)

•

\mathcal{PT}
symmetry and antiunitary symmetry with respect to
\sigma_{z}
.
Take
\mathcal{P}:=\sigma_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
to be complex conjugation, then the generalized Boltzmann factor is
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric in the sense that

\sigma_{z}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k)^{*}\,\sigma_{z}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k),\qquad\sigma_{z}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)^{*}\,\sigma_{z}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(-k),

(SJ.267)

i.e., every momentum sector is
\mathcal{PT}
symmetric. As described in more details in Sec.
G
, the standard
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry breaking transition applies here and is controlled by
\Delta(k)
as

–

if
\Delta(k)>0
, the two eigenvalues are real and distinct.

–

if
\Delta(k)=0
, the eigenvalues and eigenvectors coalesce, giving the candidate locus for an exceptional point.

–

if
\Delta(k)<0
, the eigenvalues form a complex-conjugate pair.

•

Complex symmetricity.
Let the superscript
T
denotes transposition, then teh generalized Boltzmann factor satisfies

\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k)^{T}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k),\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)^{T}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(-k).

(SJ.268)

•

Pseudo-Hermiticity with respect to
\sigma_{z}
.
Combining the
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry (or antiunitary symmetry) and complex symmetricity above, the generalized Boltzmann factor has pseudo-Hermiticity condition,

\sigma_{z}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k)^{\dagger}\,\sigma_{z}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{PT}}(k),\qquad\sigma_{z}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k)^{\dagger}\,\sigma_{z}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(k).

(SJ.269)

Pseudo-Hermiticity is not an independent property once complex symmetricity and
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry are established.

J.4
Boundary states

The TopSFF with a spatially extended topological defect is given in Eq. (
SJ.242
). Here we describe the boundary states in the folded representation, both in position space and in momentum space. Away from the parity-inversion axes, the bulk of the quantum many-body system is parity-inversion symmetric, since it commutes with the global swap-defect operator.
Along the parity-inversion axes, the parity-inversion-symmetric coupling gates of the RPM are chosen according to Eqs. (
SA.8
) and (
SA.9
) in Sec.
A.2
, subject to the constraint

\mathrm{SWAP}\,w(t,r)\,\mathrm{SWAP}=w(t,r)
,
where
\mathrm{SWAP}
denotes the swap operator acting on the same Hilbert space as
w(t,r)
.
Coupling gates at different times are drawn independently, so discrete time translational symmetry is absent, i.e.,
w(t,r)\neq w(t^{\prime},r)
for
t\neq t^{\prime}
.
After performing the ensemble average over the random phase gates, the boundary state
\phi
and its Fourier transform, defined in Eq. (
SJ.249
), are given by

\phi(a,b)=i^{\delta_{a,1}},\qquad\widetilde{\phi}(a;k)=i^{\delta_{a,1}}\sqrt{2t}\,\delta_{n,0},

(SJ.270)

where
k=2\pi n/(2t)
with
n=0,1,\dots,2t-1
.
Note that the imaginary factors arising from the Weingarten calculus have been absorbed into the boundary state. The boundary state in Eq. (
SJ.270
) lies entirely in the momentum sector
k=0
. Hence

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}=\widetilde{\phi}(0)^{T}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\,\widetilde{\phi}(0).

(SJ.271)

In fact, in the absence of time translational symmetry, turning off the coupling gates along the parity-inversion axes of the RPM gives rise to the same boundary state as in Eq. (
SJ.270
). The presence of these coupling gates can only be detected by probing higher moments of the TopSFF and likewise of the SFF
Chan et al. 2021b
, or in the presence of time translational symmetry, see Sec.
E
.

J.5
Exact expression of TopSFF without time translational symmetry

Since the boundary state has support only at
k=0
, using the Cayley-Hamilton form in Eq. (
SF.136
), we obtain the TopSFF as

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}=\widetilde{\phi}(0)^{T}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\,\widetilde{\phi}(0)=2t\,\varphi^{T}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\varphi=2t\,g(0)\,u_{L_{\mathrm{e}}}(0),

(SJ.272)

where
\varphi=(1,i)^{T}
, and
u_{L_{\mathrm{e}}}(k)
and
g(k)
are defined in Eq. (
SF.136
).
For
k=0
, one finds

\displaystyle g(0)=

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}(0)-\mathsf{C}(0)-2\mathsf{B}(0)=\,-\mu^{4t-4}(1-\mu^{4}),

(SJ.273)

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)=

\displaystyle 2-(1+\mu^{4})\mu^{4t-4},

\displaystyle\det\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)=

\displaystyle(1-\mu^{4t})(1+\mu^{4t-4}),

\displaystyle\Delta(0):=

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)^{2}-4\det\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)=\mu^{4t-8}\Big[\mu^{4t}(1+6\mu^{4}+\mu^{8})-8\mu^{4}\Big]\leq 0\,,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\pm}(0)=

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{tr}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(0)\pm\sqrt{\Delta(0)}}{2}=1-\frac{(1+\mu^{4})\mu^{4t-4}}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Delta(0)}

Observe that for
k=0
sector,
\Delta(0)<0
for
\mu\in(0,1)
, i.e., there is not an exceptional point for
\mu\in(0,1)
, and the generalized Boltzmann factor is in the
\mathcal{PT}
broken phase with
\lambda_{\pm}
forming complex conjugate pairs. Therefore, the TopSFF with spatially extended topological defects in the absence of time translational symmetry can be evaluated as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathrm{top}}=

\displaystyle-2t\mu^{4t-4}\left(1-\mu^{4}\right)\,\frac{\lambda_{+}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}-\lambda_{-}^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}}{\sqrt{\Delta}}=-\frac{4t\mu^{4t-4}\left(1-\mu^{4}\right)}{\sqrt{-\Delta}}|\lambda|^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\,\sin\!\big(L_{\mathrm{e}}\arg\lambda\big),

(SJ.274)

\displaystyle\lambda\equiv

\displaystyle\lambda_{+}=\lambda^{*}_{-}=1-\frac{1}{2}(1+\mu^{4})\mu^{4t-4}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Delta},

\displaystyle\Delta=

\displaystyle\mu^{4t-8}\Big[\mu^{4t}\big(1+6\mu^{4}+\mu^{8}\big)-8\mu^{4}\Big]\;\leq 0\,.

where
L_{\mathrm{e}}=L/2-1
for even
L
, and
L_{\mathrm{e}}=(L-1)/2-1
for odd
L
.
\mu=e^{-\epsilon/2}
with
\epsilon\in[0,\infty)
is the RPM parameter that sets the coupling strength, with larger
\epsilon
corresponds to stronger coupling. Typical behaviour of TopSFF are plotted Fig.
S24
.

Figure S24:
TopSFF without time translational symmetry at large
q
.

\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{top}}}
in the
t
-
L
plane at
\mu=0.85
(left) and in the
\mu
-
L
plane at
t=15
(right).

J.6
Consistency check with finite-
q
result at
t=1

As a consistency check, at
t=1
,
\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{top}}}
with spatially extended topological defects reduces to

\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathrm{top}}}=-\frac{4\sqrt{1-\mu^{4}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{4}+7}}\Big(\sqrt{2(1-\mu^{4})}\Big)^{L_{\mathrm{e}}}\sin\big(L_{\mathrm{e}}\,\theta\big)\,,

(SJ.275)

where the angle
\theta
is defined such that
\cos\theta=\sqrt{(1-\mu^{4})/8}
or

\sin\theta=\sqrt{(\mu^{4}+7)/8}
.
Eq. (
SJ.275
) coincides with (i) the large-
q
expansion of the exact finite-
q
result of the TopSFF in Eq. (
SI.240
) at
t=1
, and with (ii) the exact large-
q
calculation of the TopSFF in Eq. (
SF.170
) for the Floquet parity symmetric RPM at
t=1
.

Appendix K
Temporally extended topological defects

In this appendix, we analyse TopSFF with temporally extended topological defects inserted in the spatial evolution. We consider two representative defects: a global swap defect in time reversal symmetric dynamics, and a time translation defect in Floquet dynamics. In both cases, we derive exact large-
q
expressions and the associated Thouless scaling forms for the topological free energy cost.

K.1
Temporally extended global swap defects

Here we consider the TopSFF of a temporally extended global swap defect
\hat{\mathcal{D}}=\hat{S}\otimes\hat{\mathbb{1}}
acting on
U_{\mathrm{s}}\otimes U_{\mathrm{s}}
in the spatial direction. The one-dimensional generic quantum many-body chaotic circuit
\hat{U}_{\mathrm{s}}(t,L):=\prod_{i=1}^{L}\hat{V}(t,L)
is realized generated by the space-time dual transfer matrices
\hat{V}(t,L)
[Fig.
3
(a) purple] of RPM defined in (
SA.20
), satisfying the
[\hat{V}(t,L),\hat{S}]=0
, and therefore has time reversal symmetry (TRS). We will also use the RHM with TRS, defined in Eq. (
SA.27
), to verify the universal scaling form derived below from the RPM.

Using the methods described in App.
E
and Ref.
Wu et al. 2025
, the TopSFF with temporally extended defects in the TRS RPM can be evaluated exactly in the large-
q
limit as

\overline{K_{\text{top}}}=\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\mathcal{B}^{L}\sigma_{x}\right],

where
\alpha(t)=t-1-\delta_{0,t\,\mathrm{mod}\,2}
and

[\mathcal{B}]_{mn}=1+(1-\delta_{mn})\bigl(e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}-1\bigr)

is the transfer matrix, or equivalently the Boltzmann factor, of an effective Ising model.

\sigma_{x}
is the Pauli-
x
matrix, which emerges because the global swap operator exchange the role of the ladder mode and the twisted mode described in
Wu et al. 2025
.
Thus, upon ensemble averaging, the temporally extended global swap topological defect becomes a spin-flip defect in the effective Ising description. Diagonalizing
\mathcal{B}
gives an exact expression for TopSFF with temporally extended defects in the large-
q
limit as

\overline{K_{\text{top}}}=\bigl[1+e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}\bigr]^{L}-\bigl[1-e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}\bigr]^{L}.

(SK.276)

In the Thouless double scaling limit
Chan et al. 2021a
, where

\ell\equiv L/L_{\mathrm{Th}}
is held fixed with
L_{\mathrm{Th}}=e^{\epsilon\alpha(t)}
, the TopSFF obeys

\kappa_{\text{top}}:=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}L,t\to\infty\\
\ell=L/L_{\mathrm{Th}}\end{subarray}}\overline{K_{\text{top}}}=2\sinh\ell.

(SK.277)

In the absence of topological defects, the large-
q
SFF of the TRS RPM is

\lim_{q\to\infty}\overline{K}=[1+e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}]^{L}+[1-e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}]^{L}
, see App.
C
and
Wu et al. 2025
. In the same Thouless scaling limit, the corresponding SFF scaling form is
\kappa:=\lim_{t,L\to\infty}\overline{K}=2\cosh\ell
.
Interpreting the TopSFF and SFF as partition functions, with
t
playing the role of an inverse temperature, we define the topological free energy difference as

\displaystyle\Delta F_{\text{top}}:=-\frac{1}{t}\ln\frac{\overline{K_{\text{top}}}}{\overline{K}}=-\frac{1}{t}\ln\!\left[\tanh\!\left(L\,\operatorname{atanh}e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}\right)\right],

(SK.278)

where the second equality is evaluated at the large-
q
limit.

\Delta F_{\text{top}}
has the bounds
0\leq\Delta F_{\text{top}}\leq\epsilon

where
\epsilon
is the spatial domain wall (sDW) effective tension and also controls the coupling strength of the quantum many-body system.
\Delta F_{\text{top}}
is monotonically increasing in
t
and decreasing in
L
, i.e.,
\partial_{t}\Delta F_{\text{top}}\geq 0
, and
\partial_{L}\Delta F_{\text{top}}\leq 0
.
At late time and fixed system size, we have
\Delta F_{\text{top}}=\epsilon-\tfrac{\ln L}{t}
. On the other hand, in the thermodynamic limit with large system size
L
at fixed time
t
, we have
\Delta F_{\text{top}}=\tfrac{2}{t}e^{-2L\operatorname{atanh}e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}}
, and the spatial correlation length can be extracted as
\xi=\tfrac{1}{2\operatorname{atanh}e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}}\to\tfrac{1}{2e^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}}\propto L_{\mathrm{Th}}
.
Together, we have
\lim_{L\to\infty}\lim_{t\to\infty}\Delta F_{\text{top}}=\epsilon
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{L\to\infty}\Delta F_{\text{top}}=0
, i.e., the late-time and thermodynamic limits do not commute.

We begin with the first Thouless scaling limit, where
t,L\to\infty
with the Thouless ratio
\ell\equiv L/L_{\mathrm{Th}}=Le^{-\epsilon\alpha(t)}
held fixed. In this limit, we have the free energy scaling form

\mathscr{F}_{\text{top},1}\equiv\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}L,t\to\infty\\
\ell\equiv L/L_{\mathrm{Th}}\end{subarray}}t\Delta F_{\text{top}}=-\ln\tanh\ell\,.

(SK.279)

In Fig.
S25
left panel, we provide a finite-
t
check of the corresponding Thouless scaling limit, where the finite-
t
curves at fixed
\mu=0.67
collapse onto this universal scaling function as
t
is increased. The inset shows the unscaled
\Delta F_{\text{top}}
as a function of
L
. This demonstrates that the temporally extended global swap defect measures the free-energy cost of a spatial domain wall, which becomes universal in the Thouless scaling limit.

We take the second Thouless scaling limit to be where
t
and
L
are sent to infinity, while
\tau\equiv t/t_{\mathrm{Th}}=\epsilon t/\ln L
is fixed, we can write
\Delta F_{\text{top}}=-\tfrac{1}{t}\ln L^{1-\tau}
and

\mathscr{F}_{\text{top},2}\equiv\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}L,t\to\infty\\
\tau\equiv t/t_{\mathrm{Th}}\end{subarray}}\Delta F_{\text{top}}=\begin{cases}0\quad&\tau\leq 1\,,\\
\epsilon\left(1-\tfrac{1}{\tau}\right)&\tau>1\,,\end{cases}

(SK.280)

i.e., in the Thouless scaling limit, the universal scaling form has a sharp crossover at
\tau=1
, i.e. at
t=t_{\mathrm{Th}}
. In Fig.
S25
right panel, we test this second Thouless scaling limit at fixed
\mu=0.67
, using finite-
L
data while varying
t
. As
L
is increased, the curves collapse onto the universal scaling form
\mathscr{F}_{\text{top},2}
, with a rounded finite-size crossover sharpening near
\tau=1
. The inset shows the corresponding unscaled
\Delta F_{\text{top}}
as a function of
t
, illustrating the crossover from the thermodynamic-limit regime, where
\Delta F_{\text{top}}\simeq 0
, to the late-time regime, where the topological free energy cost becomes finite.

As apparent in Fig.
S25
, the convergence to the second Thouless scaling form is slower than that of the first because the two limits have parametrically different finite-size corrections.
In the first Thouless limit, fixing
\ell=Le^{-\epsilon t}
gives
L\operatorname{atanh}e^{-\epsilon t}=L(e^{-\epsilon t}+e^{-3\epsilon t}/3+\cdots)
, so the free energy difference differ from its scaling form only by power-law corrections,

t\Delta F_{\text{top}}=\mathscr{F}_{\text{top},1}(\ell)+O(L^{-2}).

(SK.281)

at large finite system size
L
.

In contrast, in the second Thouless scaling limit, fixing
\tau=t/t_{\mathrm{Th}}=\epsilon t/\ln L
gives
e^{-\epsilon t}=L^{-\tau}
, and hence
Le^{-\epsilon t}=L^{1-\tau}
. The sharp crossover at
\tau=1
is therefore rounded over the regime
|\tau-1|\ln L=O(1)
. Correspondingly, the approach to the limiting scaling form is only logarithmic,

\Delta F_{\text{top}}=\mathscr{F}_{\text{top},2}(\tau)+O\!\left(\frac{1}{\ln L}\right),

(SK.282)

at fixed
\tau=t/t_{\mathrm{Th}}=\epsilon t/\ln L
. Equivalently, the finite-size rounding of the crossover has width
\delta\tau\sim 1/\ln L
. Thus the second scaling form suffers from logarithmically slow convergence with
L
.

Figure S25:

Scaling collapse of free energy differences for temporally extended global swap defects.

Left:
